15.1The question, precisely stated

Three major, otherwise unconnected bodies of Sanskrit intellectual work carry the name Patañjali: the Mahābhāṣya, the great commentary on Pāṇini's grammar and on Kātyāyana's vārttikas, securely dateable (via internal reference to the Greek siege of Sāketa) to roughly 150 BCE; the Yoga Sūtras, the foundational text of classical Rāja Yoga, organized around the four-fold structure of psychic power, practice, samādhi, and kaivalya; and, more obliquely, a Patañjali venerated as one of the eighteen Tamil Siddhars in the Śaiva Siddha tradition, iconographically represented — this is the detail that connects directly to this ledger's ongoing concern with Chidambaram — as a half-human, half-serpentine devotee enshrined specifically in the tradition of the Naṭarāja temple, where he is depicted alongside the sage Vyāghrapāda as one of two devotees granted the vision of Śiva's cosmic dance. The question this ledger isolates is not whether these three attributions exist — they plainly do, independently documented — but whether the tradition's own identification of all three (or at minimum the grammarian and the yogin) as a single person is treated by serious modern scholarship as historically likely, historically doubtful, or genuinely undecidable, and what follows for reading Dīkṣitar's own grammatically-dense, phonemically-deliberate compositional practice if the identification is taken seriously as a doctrinal (rather than strictly historical) claim.

15.2The evidence ledger

The grammarian Patañjali, author of the Mahābhāṣya — securely dated and independently attestedDOC
StatusAmong the most securely dateable authors in the whole of classical Sanskrit literature — the Mahābhāṣya's own internal reference to the Greek siege of Sāketa fixes it to approximately 150 BCE, and the text's content (a defense and elaboration of Pāṇini against Kātyāyana's critical vārttikas) is well attested and not in scholarly dispute as a distinct, coherent work by a single identifiable grammatical tradition, whatever that author's other identities may or may not have been.
The compiler of the Yoga Sūtras — dated with far less confidence, and increasingly treated by modern scholarship as spanning multiple centuries and, plausibly, multiple handsDOC (of the scholarly uncertainty itself)
StatusIndependently documented across multiple reference sources consulted for this ledger: the Yoga Sūtras' four constituent books show internal evidence of having been composed across a considerably longer span than a single lifetime would allow — the first three books are commonly placed in a period closer to the grammarian's own 2nd-century-BCE date, while the fourth book (Kaivalya) is dated by several authorities to as late as the 5th century CE, a gap of several hundred years that has led "authorities to credit more than one author writing under this name," in the words of one tertiary reference consulted, "although there is wide variance in opinion."
The explicit scholarly judgment that the grammarian and the Yoga-sūtra compiler are probably not the same personDOC
StatusStated directly and without hedging in more than one of the reference sources consulted for this ledger: "it is unlikely that these two works are that of the same author." This is not this project's own inference — it is a citation of the sources' own explicit conclusion, offered here precisely because this ledger's discipline (following Modules I–III and every earlier question in this series) requires reporting a source's actual stated position rather than the more devotionally satisfying identification the tradition itself often prefers.
The traditional (non-scholarly) identification, and its own stated groundsTRAD
StatusMultiple grammarian-tradition authorities across the centuries — named across the sources consulted as including Bhaṭṭojī Dīkṣita, Hari Dīkṣita, Nāgeśa Bhaṭṭa, and Kauṇḍa Bhaṭṭa (the recurrence of the surname "Dīkṣita/Dīkṣitar" among these grammarian-commentators is itself worth flagging as a naming pattern within the same broader Sanskrit scholarly lineage-culture that Muthuswāmi Dīkṣitar's own family name belongs to, though this project makes no claim of direct genealogical connection between them) — held Patañjali the grammarian to be an incarnation of the mythical thousand-headed serpent Śeṣa. In the Yoga tradition, separately, Patañjali the Yoga-sūtra compiler has been identified as an incarnation of the serpent Ananta. The traditional identification of the two Patañjalis as one person draws directly on this shared serpent-incarnation motif — both traditions independently arriving at a serpent identification is treated, within the traditions themselves, as evidence the two figures are one and the same being in different textual roles, rather than as coincidence.
Why this is TRAD rather than DOCThe serpent-incarnation motif is a devotional and iconographic convention, not a historical argument — it explains why the tradition wants the identification to be true and finds it aesthetically and theologically satisfying, but it does not constitute independent historical evidence of common authorship in the way, for instance, a shared distinctive grammatical usage across both texts might.
The Chidambaram serpent-sage Patañjali specifically, enshrined at the Naṭarāja temple's own dance-hall traditionDOC
StatusIndependently attested in the art-historical and iconographic scholarship on Chidambaram: Patañjali is named among the figures depicted on the temple's gopuram sculptural programme, alongside the sage Vyāghrapāda, in the temple's own traditional account of who was granted darśana of Śiva's Ānanda Tāṇḍava at Chidambaram — the same temple whose eastern gopuram carries the full 108-karaṇa sculptural cycle discussed in Question XIV of this ledger. Modern scholarship on Patañjali's iconography (a named specialist lecture consulted for this ledger traces "the development of Patañjali's iconography, starting with early representations as a two-armed half-human half-serpentine devotee of Śiva in the tradition of the Naṭarāja Temple at Cidambaram") treats this specifically Chidambaram-associated visual identity as historically and art-historically real and traceable, independent of whether the "three Patañjalis are one person" identity-claim itself is historically sound.

15.3Why this stays open, and why the disputed status is itself the more interesting finding than either resolution would be

The evidentiary situation here differs in an instructive way from most of the other questions in this ledger. This is not a case where evidence is simply missing (as with Question I's composer-specific EEG gap) or where a claim sits at the edge of what empirical method can address (as with Question IX's rain-rāga question). It is a case where the named scholarly sources this ledger consulted have already rendered an explicit judgment — "unlikely to be the same author" — and where the tradition's own competing judgment rests openly on a devotional-iconographic motif (shared serpent-incarnation) that the sources themselves do not treat as historical evidence. The honest position is therefore not "the identity is unknown" in the sense of nobody having looked; multiple named authorities across more than a century of Indological scholarship on Patañjali's dating have looked directly at this question, and their prevailing judgment leans toward separate authorship, while the tradition's own account, resting on different grounds entirely, leans toward unity. Both positions are DOCUMENTED as positions; neither is DOCUMENTED as settled historical fact, and this ledger is not in a position to adjudicate between historical-critical method and the tradition's own devotional logic, which are, in an important sense, not even competing on the same evidentiary terms.

What can honestly be said

The single-Patañjali identification is a real, textually and iconographically documented traditional claim, resting on a specific and nameable shared motif (serpent-incarnation) rather than on vague reverence — this is a stronger traditional claim than many this ledger has encountered elsewhere, precisely because it can be stated so precisely. But the named scholarly literature on Patañjali's dating explicitly and directly judges common authorship of the grammarian and the Yoga-sūtra compiler to be unlikely, on grounds of the several-century compositional span the Yoga Sūtras themselves show internally. Both of these — the tradition's stated reason for unity, and the scholarship's stated reason for doubt — are DOCUMENTED positions. Which one a reader should find more persuasive depends on what kind of question "is this one person" is being asked as: a historical-authorship question, where the scholarly dating evidence carries real weight, or a doctrinal question about whether grammar, yoga, and dance are understood within the tradition itself as facets of one connected science, for which the identification's continued devotional and iconographic vitality — regardless of its historical status — is itself the relevant fact.

What would actually resolve, or at least further sharpen, this question
  1. A direct stylometric and doctrinal comparison between the Mahābhāṣya's grammatical method and the Yoga Sūtras' own terminology (the kind of comparison already modeled structurally in this ledger's Question VII, §7.4, for the Rāhu/Ketu authorship problem), checking specifically whether the Yoga Sūtras' first three books — the ones closer in proposed date to the grammarian's securely-fixed 150 BCE — show any measurable stylistic or conceptual continuity with the Mahābhāṣya's own approach to language and categorization, since the grammatical tradition's Mahābhāṣya is explicitly concerned with how words relate to categories and universals, a concern not obviously absent from the Yoga Sūtras' own treatment of citta-vṛtti as mental categories to be stilled.
  2. A dedicated art-historical study of when, precisely, the Chidambaram serpent-sage iconography of Patañjali first appears in the temple's own sculptural or textual record, to establish whether this specific identification (Patañjali as Chidambaram devotee) is contemporaneous with, predates, or postdates the wider grammarian/yogin identification found in the works of Bhaṭṭojī Dīkṣita and the other named commentators above — a chronological question the specialist iconographic lecture cited in §15.2 gestures toward tracing but which this ledger has not itself independently resolved.
  3. Continued explicit labelling, in this series and elsewhere, of the "one Patañjali" claim as a traditional identification resting on a specific and nameable doctrinal motif, clearly distinguished from the separate historical-authorship question the named scholarly sources have already rendered a documented (if not final) judgment on — precisely the DOC/TRAD distinction this ledger's method insists on throughout.

15.4What the Yoga Sūtras' own core mechanism actually is, stated precisely, before any claim of connection to rasa theory or to Dīkṣitar's practice

Before asking what "the essence of the Yoga Sūtras" might mean for reading Nāṭyaśāstra-descended art, it is necessary to state the text's own central definition without embellishment. The Yoga Sūtras' second aphorism — among the most frequently cited single verses in the entire text — defines yoga itself as citta-vṛtti-nirodha, the cessation (nirodha) of the fluctuations or modifications (vṛtti) of the mind-stuff (citta). The text's own subsequent verses catalogue the vṛttis into five types (right knowledge, error, conceptualization/imagination, sleep, and memory) and describe the practice of yoga as the progressive stilling of all five, such that consciousness rests in its own true, unmodified nature (svarūpe'vasthānam) rather than identifying with whichever vṛtti happens to be currently active. This is a precise, technical psychological claim about the structure and management of mental fluctuation — it is not, on its own terms, a claim about aesthetic experience, performance, or rasa at all, and any connection drawn to Nāṭyaśāstra's rasa theory (discussed at length in Question XIII of this ledger) requires an explicit bridging argument rather than an assumed equivalence.

The Yoga Sūtras' own core claim, stated on its own terms, checked against the nearest concept in Nāṭyaśāstra's rasa theory
FeatureYoga Sūtra (citta-vṛtti-nirodha)Nāṭyaśāstra rasa theory
Stated aimThe complete cessation of all mental fluctuation, so consciousness rests in its own unmodified natureThe evocation and relishing (rasāsvāda) of a specific aesthetic emotion in a qualified spectator, through a controlled sequence of vibhāva, anubhāva, and sāttvika-bhāva
Relationship to ordinary emotional fluctuation (vṛtti/bhāva)Vṛtti is precisely what is to be stilled — the practice's success is measured by the reduction, not the cultivation, of mental modificationBhāva is precisely what is to be evoked and refined — the performance's success is measured by the successful, controlled arousal of a specific rasa in the spectator, not its cessation
Where śānta rasa complicates the contrastŚānta rasa — peace, or aesthetic tranquility — is the one rasa in the later nine-rasa scheme whose sthāyi-bhāva (śama, tranquil equanimity) sits closest to the Yoga Sūtra's own stated goal of stilled fluctuation; this is also, as noted in Question XIII's own table (§13.3), the rasa whose canonical status as a ninth addition to Bharata's original eight is itself a matter of later commentarial dispute, not settled within the Nāṭyaśāstra's own original scheme.

Read plainly, the two systems are not making the same claim, and are arguably pulling in opposite directions at the level of their stated central aim — one seeks to still mental modification altogether, the other seeks to evoke a specific, controlled modification (rasa) through art. The one point of genuine, non-forced convergence is śānta rasa, and it is worth being precise about why even that convergence is partial rather than complete: śānta rasa, on the account given by its own proponents within the later rasa-theoretical tradition (most influentially Abhinavagupta, the same commentator whose Abhinavabhāratī this ledger's Question XIV already treats as the likely source of any combinatorial reasoning behind the 108 karaṇas), is not simply "the absence of emotion" achieved through art, but is itself a distinctively aesthetic experience — a relished tranquility, still framed within the spectator's aesthetic distance and pleasure in witnessing, rather than the direct, non-aesthetic cessation of mental fluctuation the Yoga Sūtras describe as a practitioner's own lived, non-representational state. The performance and the practice may point toward a structurally similar destination — a stilling, a settling, a movement away from ordinary emotional turbulence — but they reach it, on each text's own terms, by categorically different routes: one through direct contemplative withdrawal from all vṛtti, the other through a fully engaged, sensorily rich, representationally mediated aesthetic experience that happens to culminate in tranquility rather than passion.

15.5What, concretely, "getting the essence of the Yoga Sūtras" can honestly mean for reading Dīkṣitar's own practice, given everything established above

Given the disputed authorship question in §15.2–15.3 and the substantive, only partially convergent relationship between the two systems' core aims established in §15.4, this ledger is in a position to state plainly what a responsible connection between Yoga Sūtra doctrine and Dīkṣitar's own kṛti-composition can and cannot claim. It cannot claim that Dīkṣitar himself was consciously invoking a single historical Patañjali as the common author of grammar, yoga, and dance, since the historical identity question is itself unresolved by the named scholarship consulted. It cannot claim that a performed kṛti produces, or is intended to produce, citta-vṛtti-nirodha in the Yoga Sūtra's own strict sense, since that would collapse the real and substantive difference between aesthetic rasa-evocation and contemplative vṛtti-cessation established in §15.4's table. What it can claim, and what this ledger is willing to state at INF-tier, is narrower and more defensible: that Dīkṣitar's own documented compositional practice — the sustained, unhurried cauka-kāla tempo already discussed at length in Question IV of this ledger in connection with sphoṭa, the systematic avoidance of the kind of rapid, syllable-dense, emotionally agitating setting found in more overtly bhakti-ecstatic contemporaneous composers — is at minimum structurally consonant with, rather than working against, the śānta-rasa-adjacent aesthetic register in which the Yoga Sūtra's own stilling aim and Nāṭyaśāstra's rasa theory come closest to overlapping. This is a modest, carefully bounded claim, offered explicitly as this project's own structural reading rather than as a documented historical fact about Dīkṣitar's conscious intent — precisely the same evidentiary caution Question III and Question IV of this ledger already apply to comparably suggestive but ultimately unverifiable structural parallels.

What this question does not do This entry does not resolve, and does not attempt to resolve, whether the historical grammarian and the historical Yoga-sūtra compiler were one person. It reports, honestly, that the named scholarship leans toward "probably not," that the tradition's own devotional logic leans toward "yes," and that both of those are real, citable, and worth holding apart rather than collapsed into a single confident answer either way.
The bounded, defensible version of the connection, stated once more without embellishment

Three things are true simultaneously and are not in tension with one another: the historical identity of the three Patañjalis is genuinely disputed rather than settled in either direction; the Yoga Sūtras' own core aim (stilling mental fluctuation) and the Nāṭyaśāstra's own core aim (evoking a specific, relished aesthetic emotion) are substantively different projects that converge only partially, and only at the specific, itself-disputed point of śānta rasa; and Dīkṣitar's own documented, unhurried, gamaka-dense compositional idiom is structurally consonant with the aesthetic register at which that partial convergence occurs, which is a real but modest and carefully bounded observation, not a claim that Dīkṣitar was consciously practicing or invoking Patañjalian yoga through his music.

Tab 16 · Closing the ledger without closing the questions

समापनम्What eleven open questions, read together, actually tell us

12.2A pattern across the eleven

Read individually, each question looks like a separate loose end. Read together, they sort into three distinct kinds of openness worth distinguishing explicitly, because they call for different attitudes going forward. Questions I, II, V, and VIII are empirical gaps — the relevant study, comparative reconstruction, or frequency count is describable, feasible in principle, and simply has not been done, or has been done only in preliminary form. These are the questions where "someone should run this study, or compile this count" is a genuinely actionable next step, not a rhetorical flourish — and VIII in particular is among the cheapest in the whole ledger to close, since it requires compilation and arithmetic against a source already linked in this revision (the medieval.org index) rather than new data collection.

Questions III, VII, and X share a different shape: each depends on what a two-century-old, largely oral-transmission record can still tell us, and each has a plausible but currently unconfirmed path to at least partial resolution through primary or near-primary sources (a manuscript, a notated compilation, a living performance lineage) that this project has simply not yet consulted. Question IV stands alone as an interpretive-distance gap — a philosophical doctrine and a cognitive-science research program that have never been asked to speak to each other, requiring two separate pieces of work chained together before it could resolve at all. Question IX is the ledger's deliberate outlier, included to mark the boundary of the method itself: not every traditional claim reduces cleanly to an empirical hypothesis awaiting a study design, and pretending otherwise would be its own kind of overclaim. Questions VI and XI, finally, are not about Dīkṣitar's period at all — they are questions about this project's own method and evidentiary chain, and are, not coincidentally, the two most directly and cheaply resolvable of the eleven, since resolving them requires only intellectual honesty and access to existing primary compilations, not new fieldwork or laboratory time.

12.3What doesn't need a question mark

It is worth stating plainly, in closing, what this ledger of open questions does not put in doubt. The DOCUMENTED-tier structural findings across Modules I–III — the rāga-mudra embeddings, the vibhakti-case mapping across the Navāvaraṇa set, the tāḷa-completism of the Navagraha cycle, the sandhi and samāsa analysis of specific surviving lines, the Kādi-lineage self-declaration in Dīkṣitar's own texts — do not depend on any of the eleven questions above resolving in any particular direction. Those findings rest on textual and musicological facts that are independently checkable now, by anyone willing to look at the compositions and the cited sources — even granting Question XI's point that "checkable" currently means "checkable against secondary literature, now linked directly in this revision's source directory," not yet "verified against primary transmission." What remains open is specifically the further, more ambitious layer of claims — about neural effects, about historical tonal lineage, about compositional intent at the level of an eleven-kṛti cycle, about testable cognitive mechanisms, about clinical efficacy, about statistical significance of a noticed pattern, about the limits of empirical testability itself, and about this project's own interpretive and citational apparatus. Keeping that layer visibly separate from the documented layer beneath it is, as it was in Modules I–III, the entire discipline this series has tried to practice rather than merely claim.

12.4Which questions depend on others resolving first

Read as a set rather than as eleven independent items, several of these questions are not actually independent — resolving one changes what evidence is available for another, or one's resolution is a logical prerequisite for trusting another's. Mapping these dependencies matters because it changes the practical order in which a research program addressing this whole ledger would want to proceed, rather than working through the eleven in numerical order as this ledger presents them for expository reasons alone.

Cross-question dependencies
QuestionDepends on, or is strengthened by, resolving firstNature of the dependency
III (ka→śrī intent)XI (source-chain integrity)If Question XI's audit finds the Śrīramkumar lecture-demonstration source for Question III is itself well-grounded in primary transmission, confidence in Question III's documented layer rises; if the audit finds otherwise, Question III's evidentiary floor is shakier than it currently appears.
VII (Rāhu/Ketu authorship)XI (source-chain integrity)The "likely completed by followers" attribution tradition Question VII investigates is exactly the kind of claim Question XI's audit method is built to trace — resolving XI's general method first would sharpen VII's specific investigation.
X (missing tāḷas)XI (source-chain integrity)Directly and explicitly — §10.5 already runs a miniature version of Question XI's audit on this exact question, and a full-scale Pradarśinī consultation (Question XI's top resolution item) would likely resolve Question X outright as a side effect.
VIII (Bhairavi signature)No other question directly, but shares infrastructure with any future corpus-wide compilation effortA full rāga-frequency count compiled to answer Question VIII, using the medieval.org index already linked above, would also supply raw data useful for auditing several other frequency-dependent claims (such as the "ghana-rāga favouring" pattern noted informally across Module II's index) not currently posed as standalone questions in this ledger.
I (EEG gap)Independent of the other tenResolvable entirely on its own terms through new data collection; does not require any other question in this ledger to resolve first.
IX (rain-rāga)Independent, but its resolution method partly informed by how IV (sphoṭa) treats the empirical/symbolic boundary questionBoth IV and IX turn on the same underlying methodological question — when a traditional claim is or is not amenable to empirical test at all — though they apply it to unrelated subject matter (cognition versus meteorology).

The clearest practical implication of this dependency map is that Question XI is not merely one item among eleven but sits partly upstream of three others (III, VII, X) — a research program working through this ledger efficiently would have good reason to prioritize the Pradarśinī consultation Question XI recommends earlier rather than later, since its results would feed directly into resolving or sharpening three further questions rather than only its own.

12.5Ranked by resource cost, separately from ranked by importance

A separate and equally useful way to sort the eleven questions is by how much time, access, or expense actual resolution would require — a dimension distinct from how important or how currently uncertain each question is, and one worth keeping visibly separate so that "hardest to resolve" is not silently conflated with "matters most."

The eleven questions ranked from lowest to highest resource cost to meaningfully advance
RankQuestionWhy it sits here
1 (lowest cost)VI — Kośa-framework reliabilityRequires only independent readers and existing compiled data (Module II's own index) — no fieldwork, travel, or archival access needed.
2XI — Source-chain integrityRequires access to an available (if not widely held) primary text and careful bibliographic tracing — more demanding than VI but still achievable through library/archival access rather than new data collection.
3VIII — Bhairavi signatureRequires compiling an existing but scattered catalogue (the medieval.org index linked above is the starting point) into a usable frequency table — labour-intensive but not requiring new fieldwork, laboratory access, or specialist equipment.
4X — Missing tāḷasRequires either the same Pradarśinī access as Question XI or direct outreach to performing institutions — moderate cost, contingent on archival or institutional access.
5II — Sāman-svara mappingRequires specialist philological and acoustic-phonetic expertise across multiple Vedic recensions — a genuinely scholarly undertaking, though not requiring laboratory equipment.
6VII — Rāhu/Ketu authorshipRequires either stylometric software/expertise or paramparā oral-history fieldwork — moderate-to-high cost depending on which resolution path is chosen.
7IX — Rain-rāga testabilityThe historical-audit path requires cross-referencing performance records against meteorological archives across potentially many decades and locations — high labour cost for what may be a small and incomplete surviving record.
8III — Ka→śrī intentionalityThe comparative-cycle-survey path is achievable with existing data (as §3.5 already partially demonstrates); the primary-document-discovery path is high-cost and low-probability, pulling the overall ranking upward.
9V — Rāga-chikitsā replicationRequires a full clinical trial — participant recruitment, ethical approval, extended follow-up — a substantial undertaking by any research-cost standard.
10I — EEG composer-specificityRequires laboratory access, EEG equipment, participant recruitment, and specialist signal-processing expertise — a genuine empirical study with real infrastructure cost.
11 (highest cost)IV — Sphoṭa/gamaka cognitive parallelRequires two chained pieces of work — a full music-cognition study of the kind Question I also needs, plus a separate, careful interdisciplinary argument connecting any finding back to a specific and internally disputed classical doctrine — the most demanding combination in the ledger.

Placing this ranking beside the importance-oriented reading in §12.2 above is itself instructive: the two cheapest questions to resolve (VI and XI) are also, per §12.4's dependency analysis, among the most consequential for the evidentiary confidence of several other questions in the ledger — meaning the single most efficient next step for anyone taking this ledger seriously as a research agenda is not the most dramatic-sounding item (Question I's EEG gap, or Question IV's cognitive-philosophical parallel) but the comparatively unglamorous work of an inter-rater reliability check and a library visit to consult a 1904 notation compilation.

The one-sentence version

Three modules of real, checkable scholarship sit underneath eleven real, currently unanswerable questions — and the honest and useful thing to do with the eleven is not to quietly resolve them by rhetorical momentum, but to write down, as precisely as this ledger has tried to, exactly what would have to happen for each one to stop being open — including, per Question XI, what would have to happen for this project's own evidentiary chain to stand on firmer ground than it currently does, and, per this closing section, which of the eleven would most efficiently be tackled first.
